What it shows (our notes): Page contents: 3D comic "Duel of the Fates!" (writer Steve White, art Will Sliney, colors Digikore, letters Andrew James; viewed): on Tatooine, young Anakin and Sebulba trade boasts; in the Coruscant underworld, Darth Maul watches swoop riders. (hand-read)
> Throughout the scenario, we’ll try to give you cues, and there should be a different emotional chain rattled in each encounter. In the first, we have Lora, a frightened Jedi afraid of failing what remains of her order. If you can squeeze out some tears, it wouldn’t be inappropriate. In the second The Eye Page 6 encounter, we have a panicked Klide who seems to only want to survive. We have the menacing hitmen on speeders, the malformed mulissiki, and the loss of two more familiar faces, in encounters three through five. Everything is wrong. Everything is pushing to an inevitable confrontation, and my friend, let me tell you – there can be no more appropriate time to crank “Duel of the Fates” up to 11 than during the combat with Markus and his minions (you didn’t think he’d be alone, did you?). Finally, though, comes the last decision – and one final celebration, the night before our heroes go their separate ways. Nyub-nyub, my friends. Nyub. Nyub.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #136 (2012); Page contents: 3D comic.
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Star Wars Roleplaying Game Revised Core Living Force Campaign Adventures Among the Stars Year 5 515 EndTimes The Eye (text copy `Star_Wars_Roleplaying_Game_Revised_Core_Living_Force_Campaign_Adventures_Among_the_Stars_Year_5_515_EndTimes_The_Eye_pdf_eb445617f0`).)

## Star Wars Insider #140 (2013)

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): Page contents: "Jedi No More?": the Ahsoka finale arc. The opener at Cato Neimoidia shows her "in her prime" saving Anakin (crop-verified); the Jedi Temple bombing and her framing; the Council plays politics; Barriss Offee revealed as the head conspirator, believing the Order corrupt; Tarkin's military rises; the Republic courtroom mirrors the Death Star. (hand-read)
  — Source: Star Wars Insider #140 (2013). POV: Page contents, about Darth Vader / Anakin Skywalker.
Our notes: `notes/materials/038-star-wars-insider/140-ins140.md:100`

> We wanted to challenge her, so the opening sequence in the first part of the arc shows her flying toward Cato Neimoidia and the action is all about showing her in her prime, and how she is now capable. Anakin shouldn't have to worry about her, because she's the one that saves him. She has a bit of an edge to her, not unlike Anakin.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #140 (2013).
Note (our notes, around the quote): Ahsoka Tano; Darth Vader (Anakin Skywalker) — entry/section: Filoni on Ahsoka in her prime: ""We wanted to challenge her, so the opening sequence in the first part of the arc shows her flying toward Cato Neimoidia and the action is all about showing her in her prime, and how she is now capable. Anakin shouldn't have to worry about her, because she's the one that saves him. She has a bit of an edge to her, not unlike Anakin." —Dave Filoni, Star Wars Insider 140, p. 19" Source: *Star Wars Insider #140* (2013), scan p.19

> Freeze-framing reveals Vader’s arms to be robotic, as are his legs, and the 265 Chapter VII: Demons and Angels vertebrae in his neck can be seen to be artificial as well, implying that his neck was broken in his tumble into the volcano, perhaps explaining his iron-lung necessity due to complete paralysis. Those wishing to know more on this topic should consult the Star Wars Technical Commentaries, which features an in-depth medical examination on Vader’s injuries by Dr. Curtis Saxton (http://www.theforce.net/swtc/injuries.html). The Return of the Jedi novelisation contained numerous references to Anakin’s past that did not appear in any of the scripts. Obi Wan tells of The Duel: “ ‘You should not think of that machine as your father…When I saw what had become of him, I tried to dissuade him, to draw him back from the dark side. We fought…your father fell into a molten pit. When your father clawed his way out of that fiery pool, the change had been burned into him forever—he was Darth Vader, without a trace of Anakin Skywalker. Irredeemably dark. Scarred. Kept alive only by machinery and his own black will.’ ” 575 Finally, Vader’s last thoughts as he lays dying make another reference to being burned by lava: “These were memories he wanted none of, not now.
— Source: Star Wars Insider #143 (2013).
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from novels tmp lucas bts kaminski secret history (text copy `novels_tmp_lucas_bts_kaminski_secret_history_pdf_45a5e53edc`).)

What it shows (our notes): Page contents: "Hero or Villain?" Q2 continued: Bohnhoff (Anakin and Palpatine most responsible), Wheatley (Windu's "too dangerous to be left alive" echoes Palpatine on Dooku), Windham ("It's all George Lucas's fault"; Qui-Gon could have left him on Tatooine), Hearne. (hand-read)
> Mace then had to face the Sith alone. Darth Sidious and Mace Windu engaged in a fierce duel in the Chancellor’s office. The battle waged on until, finally, Palpatine sensed Skywalker’s arrival and threw the fight, allowing Windu to disarm him and forcing him to yield. Skywalker walked in to see these events unfolding, and Sidious unleashed a powerful barrage of Force lightning against Windu. Sidious ©Lucasfilm, LTD. The Jedi Order 25 used Windu’s reliance on the art of Vaapad and its tapping into negative energies against him, influencing Windu’s thought processes on the matter at hand. Within no time, Windu’s tune had changed, from wanting to place the Sith Lord under arrest to fearing he was too dangerous to be left alive. Skywalker, seeing this change, became very conflicted; he wanted to honor his duty as a Jedi, but if he let Master Windu kill Palpatine, his wife Padme’s life would be forfeit. Palpatine compounded Skywalker’s feelings by playing the helpless captive. Just as Windu was about to land a coup de grace on the Sith Lord, Anakin acted, slicing Mace Windu’s lightsaber hand off at the wrist. This gave Palpatine all the chance he needed to end the fight.
>
> It's all George Lucas's fault
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
— Source: Star Wars Insider #145 (2013). POV: Page contents, about Darth Vader / Anakin Skywalker.

> Like all villains who are more than cardboard, Vader's both villain and victim. He's a mass-murderer on an unimaginable scale, but we can see how he got there—he was used and led astray (by both the Sith and the Jedi) and then lashed out in a fury and made awful choices.
>

## Star Wars: Legacy (Dark Horse, 2006–2010) single issues #20–#50, hand-read

> WELCOME TO THE FUTURE . . .
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> After the deaths of Palpatine and Vader, Luke Skywalker's New Jedi Order became the agent of peace in the galaxy. The remnant of the Empire made peace with a new Republic. The Republic fell, but was replaced by a Galactic Alliance, which allied itself with the Imperial Remnant and others to defeat the alien invaders.

  — Source: Star Wars: Empire's End #1 (Oct 1995).
Our notes: `notes/materials/041-dark-empire/ee-01.md:172`

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): How the Emperor dies: not in a Jedi duel. After killing Rayf he is "DRAINED" (p20) and openly baits Luke to kill him. Luke offers surrender. The Emperor lunges for Anakin (p21), and Han shoots him with a blaster: "GOT HIM!" The Emperor says "THE CORELLIAN HAS KILLED ME" (p21).
  — Source: Star Wars: Empire's End #2 (Nov 1995).
Our notes: `notes/materials/041-dark-empire/ee-02.md:163`

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): Feat (Brand, spirit capture): the decisive feat of the saga. Badly hurt, Brand intercepts the Emperor's spirit as it moves into Anakin, takes it into himself ("HIS SPIRIT IS MY PRISONER") and holds it until he dies, taking Palpatine with him (p22–23). A column of light rises from his body (p23).
  — Source: Star Wars: Empire's End #2 (Nov 1995).
Our notes: `notes/materials/041-dark-empire/ee-02.md:188`

Context: | # | Who | Feat | Where | Conditions / caveats |

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): Emperor: deadly in output to the very end. In EE #2 alone he finds Anakin with the Oracle Stone (p6), sees through Vima (p11), mind-tricks Lady Kira (p16), and in one fight floors Leia, downs Brand and kills Rayf (p18–20). But every source agrees his body is failing: the physician (EE #1 p18), the Sith spirits (EE #2 p5), his adept (EE #2 p18) and he himself ("THE BATTLE HAS DRAINED ME", EE #2 p20). (ANALYSIS) EE is a race against his own decay. No hero ever overpowers him at full strength.
  — Source: Star Wars: Empire's End (#1–2, Oct–Nov 1995): series summary and scaling notes.
Our notes: `notes/materials/041-dark-empire/ee-summary.md:58`

> "I'll say the work in the *Dark Empire* series is very impressive. I look with envy at the crisp, stylized drawings by Cam Kennedy, the rich color and painterly effects Dave Dorman gets into his work."
— Source: Dark Empire trade paperbacks: extra (non-story) material, hand-read.
Context: McQuarrie foreword (art and career, no lore): he says he was "the first person to see Darth Vader, a lightsaber, Artoo-Detoo" after Lucas, and he started in "December 1974, twenty years ago". He mentions talking with Rick McCallum about prequel work and admires Moebius and Ken Steacy. On the book:
Our notes: `notes/materials/041-dark-empire/tpb-extras.md:79`

> "With the birth of Leia and Han's third child, Anakin Solo, Luke Skywalker feels a surge of hope for the future of the Jedi Knights."
— Source: Dark Empire trade paperbacks: extra (non-story) material, hand-read.
Context: TPB crawl (img 006). It is reworded from the single issue and adds explicit clone/Anakin intent:
Our notes: `notes/materials/041-dark-empire/tpb-extras.md:101`

> "Since his last clone body is rapidly nearing its end, drained by the volcanic anger he feels toward the Jedi and Rebel Alliance, Palpatine is determined to rebuild his line of clones and turns to young Anakin to do so."
— Source: Dark Empire trade paperbacks: extra (non-story) material, hand-read.
Context: — Empire's End TPB, "Rage of the Emperor" (img 006)
Our notes: `notes/materials/041-dark-empire/tpb-extras.md:112`

> life span [is] shortened by
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
— Source: Dark Empire trade paperbacks: extra (non-story) material, hand-read.
Note (our notes, around the quote): Differences from EE #2 p3 (ANALYSIS): the single issue says the body's "life span [is] shortened by" his anger. The TPB says "drained by", and it adds the explicit plan to rebuild the clone line through Anakin. It also calls the Galaxy Gun "Umak Leth's" weapon and gives it "deadly precision".
Our notes: `notes/materials/041-dark-empire/tpb-extras.md:120`

> "The Emperor is back — and this time he's out for blood!"
>
> "Following his battle with Luke Skywalker in *Dark Empire II*, Emperor Palpatine was forced to occupy an inferior clone-body. That body is now aging rapidly, and Palpatine's final, irrevocable end is near unless he can infuse his line of clones with genetic material from a Jedi bloodline. His target: Han and Leia's youngest child, Anakin. His weapon: the unthwarted Galaxy Gun."
>
> "Armed with their charge to protect Leia's children, Luke and the Jedi vow to end Palpatine's dark reign for good and restore peace to the galaxy."
— Source: Dark Empire trade paperbacks: extra (non-story) material, hand-read.
Context: Back cover (img 057). It states the clone-body mechanic and the Jedi-bloodline need outright:
Our notes: `notes/materials/041-dark-empire/tpb-extras.md:122`

> Though Vader has risen to a position of great power, his ascension has not been without sacrifice. > Please keep me sane…
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> Routine maintenance complete. All neural links have been reset. You should be operating at peak efficiency for another --
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> Schedule the next procedure.
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> Give me my helmet. I am being summoned.
— Source: Star Wars: Darth Vader and the Lost Command #1 (Dark Horse, Jan 2011): hand-read notes.
Note (our notes, around the quote): pp. 04–06: Vader suspended out of his suit in a medical chamber, with surgical droids. "Please keep me sane…" "Beginning reassembly now." You should be operating at peak efficiency for another --" Vader, gripping his scarred head: "Schedule the next procedure." "Give me my helmet. I am being summoned."

> It is still the early days of the Empire, and Emperor Palpatine's apprentice Darth Vader is still being tested and evaluated by his Master—and others. Alone with his own doubts, Vader wonders if he made the right choice in betraying the Jedi and becoming a Sith.
>
> Moff Tarkin's son, an admiral for the Imperial fleet, disappeared along with his command months prior.
>
> Vader and battalions of the 501st Stormtrooper Legion attack and conquer the locals. But then Vader encounters Lady Saro, the system's religious leader . . .
— Source: Darth Vader and the Lost Command #2 (Dark Horse, Feb 2011): page-by-page notes; p. 02 crawl: >.

But then Vader encounters Lady Saro, the system's religious leader . . ." Indicia: #2, February 2011.

> Atoa, the Ghost Nebula.
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> Expose the cranial cavity.

> Lord Vader will see that my offer is reasonable. In time.

> Proceed through the sternum and extract the lungs.

> Your species is remarkable.
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> Captain Shale, did you know Atoans have no hearts?
>
> From our feet to our fingertips, we have a *thousand* hearts. They make us faster and stronger than your kind.
>
> You also bleed to death much more quickly. Now, you will tell me how you learned to speak Basic, or I will seek out each of your hearts and crush them between my fingers.
— Source: Darth Vader and the Lost Command #2 (Dark Horse, Feb 2011): page-by-page notes; p. 04.

Now, you will tell me how you learned to speak Basic, or I will seek out each of your hearts and crush them between my fingers." Specimen tanks hold bodies.

>
> I am my people's shaman. Their spiritual protector.

> I will help you find Garoche Tarkin … and in exchange, you will declare me *queen* of the Ghost Nebula.
> [fragment — full passage not on file]

> If you've come to kill me, I could not ask for a more beautiful place to die.
>
> The Empire collects specimens from every conquered planet. Test subjects for our experiments with biological and chemical weapons.
>
> I agree to your terms. Once we find Admiral Tarkin, this system is yours. But you will swear loyalty to the Emperor.

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): (ANALYSIS) Plot: Shale's mutiny is meant to save Garoche Tarkin. Tarkin has gone native under Saro. Saro has been using Vader to kill her rivals. The Padmé apparition (p. 24) is probably Atoan/Saro-induced; the issue doesn't say.
  — Source: Darth Vader and the Lost Command #4 (Dark Horse, Apr 2011): page-by-page notes.

> Darth Vader was sent to the Atoan system to locate a missing Star Destroyer and its commander, Admiral Garoche Tarkin, the son of the Emperor's most loyal ally.
>
> Following an encounter with the mysterious Lady Saro, who promised to help locate Tarkin in exchange for being named queen of the Atoan system, Vader embarked on a war of conquest, destroying the native defenses and setting the stage for Saro's reign. But still Tarkin was not found.
>
> Then came the betrayal by Vader's second in command, Captain Shale, and the stormtroopers loyal to him. Vader and his last squad of trusted troopers from the 501st were ambushed on the Atoan crystal planet, and Vader was captured by Shale, Saro, and the *traitor* Garoche Tarkin . . .
— Source: Darth Vader and the Lost Command #5 (Dark Horse, May 2011): page-by-page notes; p. 02 crawl: >.

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): (ANALYSIS) Vader openly confesses killing Padmé and (he believes) their child, and admits self-hatred. It is a key Legends characterization beat for c. 19 BBY. The "forgiveness" mirrors Saro's method with Garoche (LC#4 p. 23: "She *forgave* me").
  — Source: Darth Vader and the Lost Command #5 (Dark Horse, May 2011): page-by-page notes.
Context: p. 16: Vader: > "Yes. I killed you. I killed *you*. I killed Anakin. I killed the child you carried. And I *hate* myself for all that I have done." The figure: "Lord Vader..." The face is part-Padmé, part-withered: > "...I *forgive* you." A tear runs down Vader's scarred cheek.
Our notes: `notes/materials/042-lost-command/lc-05.md:25`

> Where have you gone???
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> I am right here, Lord Vader. Still in your heart, in your head.

- Note (summary from our notes, not a quotation): Plot: c. 19 BBY. Palpatine sends Vader to the Atoan system in the Ghost Nebula to find Admiral Garoche Tarkin, Moff Tarkin's son. Lady Saro, High Priestess of Atoa, offers to help in exchange for being made queen. Vader's conquest kills her rivals. Captain Shale (Garoche's friend) mutinies. Garoche has gone over to Saro, who is carrying his child. Saro's illusions of Padmé break Vader down. He collapses the cathedral on everyone, survives, and Palpatine lets Wilhuff Tarkin believe natives killed his son.
  — Source: 042 Lost Command: summary of strongest findings.
Our notes: `notes/materials/042-lost-command/summary.md:3`

> “Yes,” Khabarakh said. “That is his flagship, the Chimaera. I remember the day that the Lord Darth Vader first brought him to us,” he added, his mewing voice becoming reflective. “The Lord Vader told us that his duties against the Emperor’s enemies would now be taking his full attention. That the Grand Admiral would henceforth be our lord and commander.” He made a strange, almost purring sound deep in his chest. “There were many who were sad that day. The Lord Vader had been the only one save the Emperor who cared for Noghri well-being. He had given us hope and purpose.”

What it shows (our notes): C'baoth: "Then you should be in control ... In control of yourself; in control of the people and events around you. Always."
> “There are no buts, Jedi Skywalker,” C’baoth cut him off sharply. “To be a Jedi is to be a servant of the Force. I called you through the Force; and when the Force calls, you must obey.”
>
> “Yes, I know,” Luke said. “They were hunted down by the Emperor and Darth Vader.”
>
> “And one or two other Dark Jedi with them,” C’baoth said grimly, his gaze turned inward. “Dark Jedi like Vader. I fought the last of them on—” He broke off, shaking his head slowly. “So long ago.”
>
> “Justice.” C’baoth’s lip twisted. “Do not look to lesser beings for justice, Jedi Skywalker.” He slapped himself twice on the chest, two quick movements of his fingertips. “We are the true justice of this galaxy. We two, and the new legacy of Jedi that we will forge to follow us. Leave the petty battles to others, and prepare yourself for that future.”
— Source: Dark Force Rising — notes part 5: ch. 12–14. POV: C'baoth, about Darth Vader / Anakin Skywalker.

What it shows (our notes): Mara's recurring dream: Vader and Luke turn on the Emperor, who "raised his hands, sending cascades of jagged blue-white lightning at his enemies. Both men staggered". "YOU WILL KILL LUKE SKYWALKER!" She has had "The same dream ... for five years now."
> She looked back at him, wanting desperately to turn away from the coming disaster but unable to move. A thousand thoughts and emotions flooded in through that gaze, a glittering kaleidoscope of pain and fear and rage that spun far too fast for her to really absorb. The Emperor raised his hands, sending cascades of jagged blue-white lightning at his enemies. Both men staggered under the counterattack, and Mara watched with the sudden agonized hope that this time it might end differently. But no. Vader and Skywalker straightened; and with another roar of rage, they lifted their lightsabers high—
>
> Mara Jade performed dozens of successful missions for the Emperor, earning his appreciation and his occasional indulgence for the missions she failed. One such was her mission to assassinate Luke Skywalker, the Jedi hero of the Rebellion who destroyed the first Death Star and escaped Darth Vader's clutches on Bespin. Mara intercepted Skywalker on Tatooine, in the desert palace of the depraved Hutt crime lord Jabba, but was prevented from fulfilling her mission. Skywalker escaped, and though the Emperor was disappointed, he ordered Mara to give up the chase for the time being. When the Emperor next contacted her, he was dyingslain by Darth Vader at the Battle of Endor. His final telepathic command thundered in Mara Jade's mind: "You will kill Luke Skywalker." But in the turmoil following news of the Emperor's death, it was all Mara could do to arrange passage away from Imperial Center without being captured and executed by the Emperor's Director of Intelligence, Ysanne lsard. Mara fled to the anonymity offered by the Outer Rim Territories. Though the Emperor's final command still pounded in her skull, she managed to live a relatively quiet life-with the occasional wild adventure brought about by her past position in the Empire. Eventually, she joined the smuggler baron Talon Karrde and served with him as a mechanic and, after a time, as an advisor.
>
> The same dream ... for five years now.
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
— Source: Dark Force Rising — notes part 7: ch. 18–21.

What it shows (our notes): > "Here, under the command of possibly the greatest military mind the Empire had ever seen." — Heir, ch. 1 (Pellaeon POV)
> Particularly not if the pursuers were from the Rebellion.” Taking a deep breath, he straightened in his chair and, for the first time since Pellaeon had entered, opened his glowing red eyes.
>
> Pellaeon returned the other’s gaze without flinching, feeling a small flicker of pride at the achievement. Many of the Emperors top commanders and courtiers had never learned to feel comfortable with those eyes. Or with Thrawn himself, for that matter. Which was probably why the Grand Admiral had spent so much of his career out in the Unknown Regions, working to bring those still-barbaric sections of the galaxy under Imperial control. His brilliant successes had won him the title of Warlord and the right to wear the white uniform of Grand Admiral—the only nonhuman ever granted that honor by the Emperor.
>
> Ironically, it had also made him all the more indispensable to the frontier campaigns.
>
> Pellaeon had often wondered how the Battle of Endor would have ended if Thrawn, not Vader, had been commanding the Executor.

> But there was a mental pattern the Emperor had taught her long ago, a pattern for those times when he’d wanted his instructions hidden even from Vader. If she could just clear her mind enough to get it in place—
>
> Through the turmoil came a sudden jolt of pain. “Do not attempt to hide your thoughts from me, Mara Jade,” C’baoth admonished her sharply. “You are mine now. It is not right for an apprentice to hide her thoughts from her master.”
>
> “You disappoint me, Mara Jade,” C’baoth said, shaking his head. “Do you truly believe I need to hear your thoughts in order to read your heart? Like the lesser peoples of the galaxy, you seek my destruction. A foolish notion. Did the Emperor teach you nothing about our destiny?”

What it shows (our notes): Car'das background: he once ran "a network of smugglers and information brokers who had serviced everyone from the Hutts to the highest levels of the Imperial court". He was with Thrawn "to the edge of Chiss space … back before the Clone Wars". Vader since Yavin has "grown a whole lot darker".
> It hadn’t always been that way. Once, Car’das had been head of an organization that had spanned the galaxy, a network of smugglers and information brokers who had serviced everyone from the Hutts to the highest levels of the Imperial court. Car’das himself had been to the edge of Chiss space with Thrawn, back before the Clone Wars had savaged the Republic. He’d worked with the young commander, watching as he defeated forces far larger than his own. Later, as Car’das’s organization grew, he’d had many occassions to speak directly with some of the most powerful men in Palpatine’s new Empire. In those days, standing before Darth Vader would have been little more than an unusually interesting day.
>
> to the edge of Chiss space … back before the Clone Wars
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> But Vader was even worse. Ever since Yavin, every report Car’das had picked up had indicated that the appropriately titled Dark Lord of the Sith had grown a whole lot darker. The thought of asking him for anything, let alone six Star Destroyers, was something Car’das’s mind wasn’t up to.
— Source: The Last Command: notes part 11, ch. 27 (end) to ch. 29, plus front of back matter; Car'das background: he once ran.

> Pash and his entire TIE wing (nearly 72 pilots and ships) defected
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> All the while Pash was at Vensenor, he was assembling a select group of pilots that he could trust, and more importantly, who could trust him. Certainly, his 42 The Thrawn Trilogy Sourcebook flashy tactics and piloting ability attracted a lot of attention among his fellow students, and his careful attention to the safety of his squadron made him a popular leader. Pash graduated from the Vensenor Academy in the top one percent of his class, and was immediately commissioned into the Imperial Navy. Shortly after his graduation, Pashand his entire TIE wing (nearly 72 pilots and ships) defected to the Alliance. While the particulars of this incident remain classified, it took on a nearly mythic quality within the ranks of both the Empire and the Alliance, and supposedly this incident attracted the attention of no less than LordVader himself, who personally placed a new commander aboard the Star Destroyer. Thegroup, now known as the Cracken Flight Group, was assigned to the Alliance base in theXyquinesystem, where they distinguished themselves in hit-and-fade runs against Imperial shipping. Unfortunately, an Imperial probe droid located the base atXyquine II and an Imperial attackwas imminent. The attack coincided with a delicate covert Rebel operation, and an unusually high number of important RebeloperativeswereonXyquine.Thebasewasevacuated, and Pash developed a unique code called the "Cracken Twist" to aid in their escape.
>
> After demonstrating his leadership, Pash and his pilots were stationed on the planet Generis in the Atrivis system, shortly after the Battle of Endor. The Cracken Flight Group was added to Wing Commander Varth's wing to augment the principal line of defense for the Outer Rim comm center. Then, it fell to the forces of Grand Admiral Thrawn. The comm center was a huge loss to the New Republic, as it was the communication nexus to and from the Outer Rim Territories. Now that it is under Imperial control, the New Republic has lost an important tactical asset. Pash Cracken looks the part of the brash starfighter pilot.
— Source: Thrawn Trilogy Sourcebook, notes part 3: PDF pp. 32–41 (book pp. 35–44), Ch. 2 (cont.): NR military.
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from WEG40131 Star Wars The Thrawn Trilogy Sourcebook pdf x.txt (text copy `MEGA_WEG40131_Star_Wars_The_Thrawn_Trilogy_Sourcebook_pdf_x`).)

"Pash and his entire TIE wing (nearly 72 pilots and ships) defected", and Vader reportedly "personally placed a new commander aboard the Star Destroyer". The "Cracken Twist" evacuation code at Xyquine II. Generis (Atrivis) comm center, "the communication nexus to and from the Outer Rim", fell to Thrawn. Starfighter piloting 9D+2, starfighter repair 10D+2.

> Kosk was the kind of soldier that Thrawn hoped to eliminate from the Empire's ranks. He was more interested in personal wealth and power than the greater good of the Empire. He was a liability to Thrawn and his plans. Kosk's actions gave Karrde a perfect excuse to firm up the smuggler coalition, which eventually led to Thrawn's downfall. • Lt. Reynol Kosk Type: Imperial Army Officer DEXTERITY 3D Blaster 5D, dodge 5D, grenade 4D KNOWLEDGE 2D Bureaucracy4D MECHANICAL 2D Repulsorlift operation 4D The Thrawn Trilogy Sourcebook 80 Chapter Four: Remnants of the Empire PERCEPTION 2D STRENGTH 3D+1 Brawling 4D+1 TECHNICAL 2D Move:10 Equipment: Imperial Army uniform, blaster pistol (4D), comlink, datapad, survival pack Lt. Mithel Ensign Rejlii Mithel is the son of the infamous "Mauler" Mithel one of Vader's wingmen, killed at the Battle of Yavin. Rejlii Mithel was a young officer serving aboard the Star Destroyer Chimaera as a weapons technician, specializing in the vessel's tractor beam systems. During an encounter with a New Republic vessel, he gained the notice of Grand Admiral Thrawn. The Ghtroc freighter the Chimaera was pursuing disintegrated into a bunch of highly reflective particles, fouling the targeting sensors, and freeing the X-wing starfighter hidden inside: a textbook covert shroud gambit.
— Source: Thrawn Trilogy Sourcebook, notes part 7: PDF pp. 76–87 (book pp. 79–90), Ch. 4 end; Ch. 5 The Fringe (start); Lt. Rejlii Mithel.

> "Our basic idea is to use a framing device which could potentially enclose a lot of different stories about the subject that interests us most: the Jedi. … an outlaw monastic order or secret society charged with keeping alive the memory of 'the days when Jedi were numerous and the Force was honored and respected throughout the Republic.' … would be called, most probably, The Jedi Chronicles." — Insider #157 p. 50
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> "our first ambition is to examine the period between the first and second trilogies, when Darth Vader rose to preeminence as 'Dark Lord of the Sith'. We are told that it was during this 20 year span following the Clone Wars that Vader 'hunted down and exterminated the remaining Jedi'--all except Yoda and Obi-Wan Kenobi. … we would want to focus on the greatness of the martyred Masters, their wise and ingenious use of the Force, their awesome exploits against the Empire, and ultimately, their heroic deaths." — Insider #157 p. 50
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
— Source: Star Wars Insider #157–159 (Titan, 2015): "Of Rebel Dreams and Dark Empires: The Making of a Star Wars Legend" by Michael Kogge, parts 1–3.

> THE WISDOM and self-sacrifice of Mace Windu, senior member of the Jedi Council, is legendary. In a long and adventurous career, he has repeatedly risked his life to resolve great conflicts in fairness to both sides. Windu is sober and cool-minded but is also capable of dramatic actions in the face of danger. Even experienced Jedi look to this great man in times of serious trouble.

In a long and adventurous career, he has repeatedly risked his life to resolve great conflicts in fairness to both sides. Windu is sober and cool-minded but is also capable of dramatic actions in the face of danger. Even experienced Jedi look to this great man in times of serious trouble." Wary of Qui-Gon's belief in Anakin; "senses great danger with the boy." Inset: "Time and again, Mace Windu has stood at the centre of great conflicts. His fame has only added to his negotiating skills." Republic Cruiser (red = political neutrality; diplomatic salon pods). Valorum calls the Council; Windu "little suspects the evil and danger". Lightsaber callouts (blade projection plate, blade modulation circuitry, handgrip ridges, activator, blade length adjust, radiator casing segment). DATA FILE: "The Jedi use the lightsabre as a symbol of their dedication to combat in defence, not attack, and of their philosophical concern for finely tuned mind and body skills." / "Ambassadors, mediators and counsellors, Jedi are warriors only as a last resort."

Note (our notes, around the quote): p06-07 The Growing Darkness: "LONG AGO, a prophesy foretold the coming of an individual who would "bring balance to the Force," ushering in a new era of peace following one of great instability. With the Separatist crisis, the future of the great Galactic Republic is clouded with uncertainty and the peace-keeping Jedi are too few to hold together an entire galaxy. In this age, the Sith have reappeared, their evil hidden perhaps in the heart of liberty's citadel". > draws concern from Jedi elders

But in an increasingly unbalanced world, Anakin must face ever steeper challenges to master the dangerous force that is himself." > Anakin is possibly the best pilot ever to train within the Jedi Temple.
>
> This scene is included on the DVD edition, though not in the main part of the film.  Anthony Daniels has revealed that he did shoot scenes using the See-Threepio wire-frame puppet used in Episode I, but that these were cut. One of these appears to have been an additional scene with Shmi Skywalker (occurring before her capture by sand people) and another with Padme and Threepio; the latter takes place while Anakin is looking for his mother in the Jundland wastes. In this Padme removes the droid’s battered metal coverings and cleans his endoskeleton, a mindless task she performs to take her mind off worrying about young Skywalker. These scenes have never been released.  QUOTES:  Obi-Wan (to Anakin):  ‘Why do I get the feeling you’re going to be the death of me?’  Padme:  ‘You’re not all-powerful.’  Anakin: ‘I should be. Someday I will be the most powerful Jedi ever.’  Anakin:  ‘We went into aggressive negotiations .. .’
>
> Only 20 Jedi have ever left the Order, but Anakin is coming perilously close.
>
> In Anakin's mind, it is his own lack of resolve and strength that has failed his mother and cost him so much pain. At his mother's grave, marked by a black stone, Anakin makes a silent vow to build his strength until nothing can withstand his power.
>
> As a result of his impetuous passions, Anakin has begun to lose his humanity.
>
> Anakin has started the study of lightsaber Form IV, known for its power.
>
> The pain of life-draining Sith lightning is an experience of evil that Anakin will never forget.
— Source: Star Wars Attack of the Clones: The Visual Dictionary (DK, 2002): hand-read notes; p17 Airspeeder.
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from George Lucas (Smith, Jim, 1978-) (text copy `novels_tmp_lucas_bts_jim_smith_epub_210ab9ca09`).)

Electrostaffs [KEY]: phrik alloy; "resistant to lightsaber strikes"; some "capable of neutralizing ray shields"; "Baton shaft cannot be severed by lightsaber"; head modeled after Krath droid; backup processors allow them to fight "even after they have been beheaded or sustained traumatic amputations." IG-101, IG-102; Kaleesh royal guard stance. DATA FILE: Holowan "a Republic-funded enterprise after the Clone Wars. It reintroduces the IG-series assassin droid"; "A dozen MagnaGuards, hand-picked by Grievous, help abduct Palpatine." Double Trouble: fight in pairs, adjust combat styles to opponents; "veterans of countless close-quarter engagements".

Lightsabers: "While the pure energy blade has no mass, the electromagnetically generated arc wave creates a strong gyroscopic effect that makes the lightsaber a distinct challenge to handle"; crystals "cannot be synthesized"; "A lightsaber must be assembled by hand ... The slightest misalignment will cause the weapon to detonate on activation." "Building a working lightsaber is one of the threshold tests for Jedi initiates: accomplishing the impossibly fine alignment task proves their Force sensitivity." Vader's saber "looks much like the one he used as a Jedi learner, only darker"; Luke's second saber "follows the type used by Luke's mentor Ben Kenobi". Ion blasters (Jawa ionization blasters stun droids). Blaster Weaponry: high-energy gas → plasma, "magnetic bottle effect"; "Plasma energy is dissipated as the bolt travels, limiting range". Droids: "the replication of true sentience has proven difficult and even dangerous"; droids escaping memory wipes "tend to develop identities and sentience of their own"; "In the Empire, no laws protect droids from abuse".

Note (our notes, around the quote): p38-39 Specialist Stormtroopers: selected early for specialization; "their psychological conditioning to their particular identity is so strong that a trooper almost never wishes to change his division." Snowtrooper ("self-sufficient mobile combat elements"; "can survive for two weeks in deeply frozen environments on suit battery power alone"); E-Web heavy repeating blaster; Vader oversees the occupation of Echo Base with General Veers' Blizzard Force. Scout Trooper ("Trained to an unusual degree of independence for Imperial personnel"; armored only on head and upper body). Speeder bike (light repulsorlift Imperial speeder bike; forward-reaching repulsor fields not strong enough to deflect the bike from obstacles on their own). DATA FILE: other divisions include "flying airtroopers, liquid-borne seatroopers, tunneling underminers, and Magma troopers who crush revolts on volcanic mining worlds"; scout troopers' macrobinocular viewplates. Scout trooper blaster.

Jedi utilize this talent not only to push, pull, and lift objects, but also to redirect projectiles and guide their starships through combat." Mind tricks: "Aliens with highly organized mental facilities [sic], such as Hutts and Toydarians, are naturally immune to Jedi mind tricks." JEDI SPIRITS: "The Sith Lord Darth Plagueis discovered it was possible to merge with the Force and still retain individual consciousness, but was not interested in the nonmaterial world. Jedi Master Qui-Gon Jinn succeeded, and later returned from the netherworld to pass his knowledge on to Yoda and Obi-Wan Kenobi". Dark Woman: original name "An'ya Kuro"; discovered "four-year-old Ki-Adi-Mundi".

DUEL ON MUSTAFAR: "As every step becomes more perilous and Vader's attacks more ferocious, Obi-Wan realizes that he still cares for Anakin, and that the only way he can defeat his opponent is to let go of his feelings for his former friend. When Obi-Wan releases this emotional attachment, the battle turns for the Jedi." A PROPHECY FULFILLED?: "Vader believes he has restored peace and justice to the galaxy and so brought balance to the Force ... He will not stray from this viewpoint until the last day of his life, when he finally realizes that there is a power stronger than the dark side." BURNING RAGE: "Despite his fighting skills, Vader is defeated by Obi-Wan. With both legs and an arm severed, Vader lies helpless as red-hot lava burns his flesh and sears his lungs ... Fueled by anger and hatred, Vader never relinquishes his embrace of the dark side." REBUILDING VADER: "incorporates advanced technology with arcane Sith healing techniques ... Vader constructs a new, red-bladed Sith lightsaber to replace the weapon he lost on Mustafar." Ubrikkian DD-13 surgical droid. Polis Massa: Padmé "does not survive". MORE MACHINE THAN MAN: "Vader is filled with fury, not because of his imagined role in Padmé's death, but because he feels cheated by her loss. In his incredible selfishness, Vader is completely consumed by the dark side of the Force, and his humanity becomes almost entirely irretrievable." Death Star construction under Tarkin; twins split up.

> And worst of all, in all these instances, the previous histories were denied and then covered up. It seems that with the mega-success of Star Wars, Lucas developed a complex for having to put forth the image that all had been elaborately planned by him, a years-old preparation that was finally seeing its satisfactory realization. Perhaps because he was pleased that all of his background story would finally pay off, he began to boast about how he had “detailed histories,” “voluminous notes” and “treatments on all the films”—although based somewhat on truth (he did indeed have notes on character backgrounds, as all writers do), they were gross exaggerations and very misleading, and as time went on they would grow to outright lies. A typical example comes in of interview in Alan Arnold’s book on the making of The Empire Strikes Back: “Alan Arnold: Tell me more about the overall concept of the Star Wars saga. George Lucas: There are essentially nine films in a series of three trilogies… AA: How much is written? GL: I have story treatments on all nine…Originally, when I wrote Star Wars, it developed into an epic on the scale of War and Peace, so big I couldn't possibly make it into a movie. So I cut it in half, but it was still too big, so I cut each half into three parts. I then had material for six movies. After the success of Star Wars I added another trilogy… So I'm still left with three trilogies of nine films.
>
> The saga, originally the adventures of Luke Skywalker, finally evolved into the story of Darth Vader.
>
> In Lucas's original script, Obi-Wan Kenobi (Alec Guinness) survived his duel with Darth Vader. Preproduction was already underway when Lucas decided to kill Obi-Wan, whose death dramatically increased the threat of both Vader and the Death Star.
>
> the second movement of a symphony
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> Jedi was a much tougher movie to pull off
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> it was the end
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> Directing is like a war
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> first major feature shot entirely on high-definition videotape
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> In IV, people didn't know whether Vader was a robot or a monster, or if there was anybody in there. This way, when you see him walk into the spaceship in Episode IV, you're going to say, 'Oh my God, that's Anakin. The poor guy is still stuck in that suit.' So the tension and drama is completely reversed.
— Source: Star Wars: The Ultimate Visual Guide (DK, 2005): Behind the Scenes, Expanding the Universe, Timeline, Index, Acknowledgments, back cover (pp. 112–144 + covers),.

Note (our notes, around the quote): p116-117 The Star Wars Saga: script begun 1972; "developed into an epic on the scale of War and Peace, so big I couldn't possibly make it into a movie. So I cut it in half, but it was still too big, so I cut each half into three parts. I then had material for six movies." "The saga, originally the adventures of Luke Skywalker, finally evolved into the story of Darth Vader." EP IV: "In Lucas's original script, Obi-Wan Kenobi (Alec Guinness) survived his duel with Darth Vader. Preproduction was already underway when Lucas decided to kill Obi-Wan, whose death dramatically increased the threat of both Vader and the Death Star." EP V: Kershner "the second movement of a symphony". Three directors. EP VI: Kasdan 2000: "Jedi was a much tougher movie to pull off"; Hamill "it was the end". Reinventing Jabba (Declan Mulholland). EP I: script begun "November 1, 1994"; "Directing is like a war". EP II: 1979 prophecy on video; "first major feature shot entirely on high-definition videotape". EP III (2005 interview) [KEY]: "In IV, people didn't know whether Vader was a robot or a monster, or if there was anybody in there. This way, when you see him walk into the spaceship in Episode IV, you're going to say, 'Oh my God, that's Anakin. The poor guy is still stuck in that suit.' So the tension and drama is completely reversed." Skywalker Ranch 1,700 → 6,500 acres.

> McQ: George had ideas about how his picture should look. In fact, I think the look of the picture was more interesting to him than the plot. The look of the great vistas, the alien lands, the structures, the spaceships, the robots, the costumes, and accessories was of fundamental interest to him. George at one time had been very interested in being an illustrator and could have been a brilliant science-fiction illustrator, I believe. He wanted to get what he could see in his mind onto the screen. When he wrote he could see these things, but descriptions of visual things in the script were nevertheless kind of sketchy. I thought that what he expected from me was the look he hoped for-the light, the textures, the excitement he saw in his mind's eye. So we'd get together and talk about the scenes that typified the scale and scope of the picture visually. I also helped visualize the robots with a painting of C-3PO and R2-D2 crossing the desert.
>
> That's great, that's fine,
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> wouldn't have to go around all day in the whole caboodle.
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> vampiric, samurai-like female character. 'I deliberately curved the Sith's lightsaber,' Power says. 'I wanted something exotic, almost Arabic.' Power's sketches were temporarily shelved when the veteran actor Christopher Lee signed on to play Count Dooku, but the art was later utilized for Asajj Ventress
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
— Source: Star Wars: The Ultimate Visual Guide (DK, 2005): Behind the Scenes, Expanding the Universe, Timeline, Index, Acknowledgments, back cover (pp. 112–144 + covers),; p118-119 Designing the Galaxy: McQuarrie: Lucas.
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Once Upon A Galaxy A Journal of the Making of The Empire Strikes Back (Alan Arnold) (text copy `novels_tmp_lucas_bts_arnold_once_upon_a_galaxy_epub_dc813096f5`).)

Note (our notes, around the quote): p118-119 Designing the Galaxy: McQuarrie: Lucas "wanted to get what he could see in his mind onto the screen". CONCEPT ART: Vader "a tall, black, majestic figure with fluttering robes, possibly wearing an exotic helmet, like a Japanese warrior, with a black silk scarf across his face ... Vader first appeared when he jumped from one spaceship to another, so Lucas agreed he should wear breathing apparatus. 'George liked the mask that I did for Vader, with the big goggles and so he said, "That's great, that's fine," and we just left it at that.'" John Mollo: actor "wouldn't have to go around all day in the whole caboodle." Developing characters: Chewbacca had fangs; C-3PO from Metropolis; lightsaber-wielding hero resembles Lucas. EXPANDED CONCEPTS [KEY]: Dermot Power's "vampiric, samurai-like female character. 'I deliberately curved the Sith's lightsaber,' Power says. 'I wanted something exotic, almost Arabic.' Power's sketches were temporarily shelved when the veteran actor Christopher Lee signed on to play Count Dooku, but the art was later utilized for Asajj Ventress". Caption: Ventress debuted in Dark Horse "Schism". Production paintings; Storyboards (Benton Jew); Animatics (Dan Gregoire; ROTS); Costume design (Iain McCaig, Trisha Biggar; Theed "clothing of paradise"; Amidala: Pre-Raphaelite + art nouveau + Tibetan and Mongolian vestments). Natividad Coruscant monorail.

> after the success of Star Wars [Episode IV] I added another trilogy
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> GL: I have story treatments on all nine. I also have voluminous notes, histories, and other material I've developed for various purposes. Some of it will be used, some not. Originally, when I wrote Star Wars, it developed into an epic on the scale of War and Peace, so big I couldn't possibly make it into a movie. So cut it in half, but it was still to big, so each half into three parts. I then had material for six movies. After the success of Star Wars but to write and finish them. Mostly, it will be a I added another trilogy but stopped there, primarily because reality took over. After all, it takes three years to prepare and make a Star Wars picture. How many years are left? So I'm still left with three trilogies of nine films. At two hours each, that's about eighteen hours of film!

Note (our notes, around the quote): p130-131 Continuing the Saga [KEY]: 1979 Lucas "after the success of Star Wars [Episode IV] I added another trilogy" and "story treatments on all nine" films, "but his more recent revelations contradict this. As guest of honour at the Star Wars: Celebration III convention in 2005, Lucas admitted to an audience that 'to be very honest with you, I never ever thought of anything that happened beyond Episode VI. It's the Darth Vader story.'" Announced 3-D animated Clone Wars and live-action series "between the events of Episode II ... and Episode III". Radio dramas (Brian Daley, NPR 1981, John Madden). HOLIDAY SPECIAL: Nov 17, 1978, CBS; animated cartoon introduced Boba Fett; "widely regarded as regrettable ... never aired again or officially released on video (although bootlegs of it do exist), so it has now assumed cult status." Ewok Adventures (1984/1985; "Lucasfilm-approved timelines place both stories before the events of Return of the Jedi"; Thomas G. Smith: walker toy "the Imperials had been there"; Emmy for effects; DVD 2005). Towani family; Teek (Niki Botelho). More Ewoks (Nelvana; Kneesaa). ANIMATED DROIDS: 13 episodes + The Great Heep; Vlix; Boonta Speeder Races; "Droids is set 15 years before A New Hope." Clone Wars 2003, Genndy Tartakovsky, Emmy; introduced Grievous before Ep III.

> The Confederacy has pulled back to the Outer Rim. Republic forces have started a series of sieges against several key worlds. Alderaan has dealings with a number of systems there -- and I need to view the situation first-hand.

>
> I will accept full responsibility for any consequences, Chancellor. I will have a cadre of my own troops accompanying me -- but I need an experienced Jedi General at my side. Preferably, someone familiar with the systems.
>
> If you insist on going, General Kenobi has extensive experience in the Outer Rim. He'll need young Skywalker's aid, of course.
>
> I'm afraid that's impossible. Anakin is on leave and Obi-Wan is tracking down Commander Ventress.

## Star Wars: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous (Sean Stewart, Del Rey 2004)

What it shows (our notes): which makes Dooku jealous. Anakin
> Oh, yes, and wasn’t everyone watching him. Even Darth Sidious, with a gleam in his eye, mentioned the boy as one strong in the Force. “Just a little piece in a great game,” his Master had said; but a stab of jealousy had gone through Dooku when Sidious lingered over the name. Skywalker, yes … The Force is strong in him.
>
> The same Anakin Skywalker who, he had learned, had recently killed a clone of Count Dooku of Serenno. Poor foolish clone. Another changeling, another Dooku abandoned by his parents, left to be chopped up by some upstart Jedi butcher in the name of a corrupt Republic.
— Source: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous (Sean Stewart, Del Rey 2004): notes part 1, front matter and Chapter 1; Sidious calls Anakin.
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Yoda (Sean Stewart) (text copy `MEGA_Legend_The_Clone_Wars_Series_Star_Wars_Clone_Wars_20_Yoda_Dark_Rendezvous_Sean_Stewart_epub`).)
(Full sentence/passage taken from the source text on file: `quick_tmp_ydr_stewart_epub`.)
Our notes: `notes/materials/050-yoda-dark-rendezvous/yda-01.md:31`

> The Force was strong in him.
>
> “Whie? No! And yes,” she said. “He is young. They are all so young. But if any of them are ready, he is. The Force is strong in him. Not so strong as in young Skywalker, but in the next level down: and between you and me, he carries it better than Anakin ever has. Such calm. Such serenity and poise; truly it is incredible in one so young.”
>
> “I think he wishes to please you very much,” the old Master said carefully.
>
> Before she could ask him what he meant, a gong sounded the hour. “Ah—my class!” Maks said, slapping one hand against her forehead horns. “I am supposed to be teaching hyperspace navigation in Tower Three.”
— Source: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous (Sean Stewart, Del Rey 2004): notes part 1, front matter and Chapter 1; Her Padawan Whie. POV: Her Padawan Whie, about Darth Vader / Anakin Skywalker.
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Yoda (Sean Stewart) (text copy `MEGA_Legend_The_Clone_Wars_Series_Star_Wars_Clone_Wars_20_Yoda_Dark_Rendezvous_Sean_Stewart_epub`).)
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Yoda (Sean Stewart) (text copy `MEGA_Legend_The_Clone_Wars_Series_Star_Wars_Clone_Wars_20_Yoda_Dark_Rendezvous_Sean_Stewart_epub`).)
Label in our notes: Leem: Yoda

What it shows (our notes): Obi-Wan and Anakin on Arkania. The Jedi Serifa Altunen resigns: "I follow the Force—not the law. I serve the people—not the Senate. I will make peace—not war." / "It is the Order that has strayed from the path."
> Carefully she took off her Jedi cloak, folded it up, and handed it to Obi-Wan. “I follow the Force—not the law. I serve the people—not the Senate. I will make peace—not war.”
>
> Serifa’s eyebrows rose. “I do not feel lost. The Force is as it always has been. It is the Order that has strayed from the path.”
— Source: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous: notes part 2, Chapters 4–5.
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Yoda (Sean Stewart), Chapter 6 (text copy `MEGA_Legend_The_Clone_Wars_Series_Star_Wars_Clone_Wars_20_Yoda_Dark_Rendezvous_Sean_Stewart_epub`).)
Our notes: `notes/materials/050-yoda-dark-rendezvous/yda-02.md:26`

> “That’s not the point,” Anakin said, still flushed and angry. “The point is, we are out here asking people to support a Republic that barely knows they exist, and backing it up with a, a police force of Jedi sworn not to care about them! And we wonder why it’s a hard sell?” He waved out through the front viewscreen. “What if Serifa is right? What if we are the ones who have lost our way? I trust what I can feel, Master. That’s what you have always taught me, isn’t it? I trust the living Force. I trust love. The ‘principle of nonattachment’ …? That’s an awfully abstract thing to pledge loyalty to.”
— Source: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous: notes part 2, Chapters 4–5; Anakin. POV: Anakin.
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Yoda (Sean Stewart), Chapter 6 (text copy `MEGA_Legend_The_Clone_Wars_Series_Star_Wars_Clone_Wars_20_Yoda_Dark_Rendezvous_Sean_Stewart_epub`).)
(Full sentence/passage taken from the source text on file: `quick_tmp_ydr_stewart_epub`.)
Our notes: `notes/materials/050-yoda-dark-rendezvous/yda-02.md:29`

> The eighteen assassin droids—the full complement that Last Call could carry in her outboard crèches—now split into four distinct groups. Four of the newcomers stayed with Asajj. Four others peeled off and headed upstairs to secure the food court area. Five were closely engaged with the two Jedi, where the one Jai had taken down lay in a heap of smoking metal. Two were operating the tactical tractor beam, holding the R2 unit pinned to the floor a safe distance away, while two others approached just close enough to pitch sonic grenades to within centimeters of the droid’s casing. The grenades went off with a churning, concussive vibration that buckled the floor underneath the R2 and made its casing writhe and ripple.
>
>
> Jai Maruk shook his head. “Confederacy assassin droids,” he bellowed, shouting to be heard over the din. He recognized them from Anakin Skywalker’s report on his mission to Jabiim. Anakin’s foes had featured fairly generic armament—usually one handheld blaster and a shoulder-mounted backup. This squad had a much more eclectic array of weapons—aside from their built-in blasters, he could see a couple of flechette launchers, sonic grenades, two flamethrowers, even two fat, hollow tubes that he was pretty sure were tactical tractor beam prototypes.
— Source: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous: notes part 3, Chapters 7–8 (Phindar).
(A fragment line was removed 2026-11-17: its words appear word for word inside the full excerpt in this entry.)
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Yoda (Sean Stewart), Chapter 7 (text copy `MEGA_Legend_The_Clone_Wars_Series_Star_Wars_Clone_Wars_20_Yoda_Dark_Rendezvous_Sean_Stewart_epub`).)
(Full sentence/passage taken from the source text on file: `quick_tmp_ydr_stewart_epub`.)
Note (our notes, around the quote): At Phindar Spaceport, the SPCB (Sentient Property Crime Bureau) confronts the droids. Whie and Scout mind-trick Major Quecks. Ventress then arrives with 18 Confederacy assassin droids ("the full complement that Last Call could carry"). They are custom-fitted for Jedi-hunting with flechette launchers, hard-sound projectors, sonic grenades and "tactical tractor beam prototypes". Jai recognizes the type "from Anakin Skywalker’s report on his mission to Jabiim".
Our notes: `notes/materials/050-yoda-dark-rendezvous/yda-03.md:11`

> For a long time Darth Sidious did not speak to him. Instead, he simply piped the breaking news story into Dooku’s holoconsole. A smiling Palleus Chuff, bruised but modestly triumphant. Long panning shots of the interior of the Phindar Spaceport: reporters pointing excitedly at spent flechettes and plasma scorch marks. Quickly patched holes in the floor and wall. Head shots of Master Yoda—“another glowing chapter in his legendary career.” Security footage of Trade Federation assassin droids running amok; two Jedi Knights bravely battling to save civilians before being cut down. Asajj Ventress, of course. A shot from external space station cams: Last Call tumbling heavily through space, accelerating, and then making a hyperdrive jump to certain doom. A state-of-the-art ship built at Dooku’s own expense—the third one she’d lost, if one counted the craft Anakin and Kenobi had stolen from her.
— Source: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous: notes part 3, Chapters 7–8 (Phindar); *Last Call* is.
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Yoda (Sean Stewart), Chapter 9 (text copy `MEGA_Legend_The_Clone_Wars_Series_Star_Wars_Clone_Wars_20_Yoda_Dark_Rendezvous_Sean_Stewart_epub`).)
(Full sentence/passage taken from the source text on file: `quick_tmp_ydr_stewart_epub`.)
Our notes: `notes/materials/050-yoda-dark-rendezvous/yda-03.md:32`

What it shows (our notes): Palpatine maneuvers Mace into sending Obi-Wan and Anakin to Vjun: "I think the world of Master Yoda, but he is very old, and perhaps not all that he once was."
> “Ah. Yes, I see. The politician is impressed with a victory on the battlefield of public relations; the military commander not so much. But I had anticipated you in this, at least somewhat. I tell you, Master Windu, I am not easy with Yoda’s situation for precisely the reasons you describe. I should be happier if you were to replace the fallen with another detail. I’m not sure who, exactly … Well, why not Obi-Wan? Didn’t I see in my last briefing that he had finished his last mission? Obi-Wan and young Skywalker. I would feel more comfortable if I knew they were on their way to Vjun. I think the world of Master Yoda, but he is very old, and perhaps not all that he once was. The idea of him facing Count Dooku alone, in the Count’s stronghold … it makes my blood run cold. Yes, Obi-Wan and Skywalker would do very well.”
— Source: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous: notes part 3, Chapters 7–8 (Phindar).
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from Yoda (Sean Stewart), Chapter 9 (text copy `MEGA_Legend_The_Clone_Wars_Series_Star_Wars_Clone_Wars_20_Yoda_Dark_Rendezvous_Sean_Stewart_epub`).)

> Obi-Wan shrugged it off, nettled. “I don’t know why I bother sticking my neck out for you.” He busied himself checking out a course for Vjun. “Especially since I feel, with every nerve in my body, that someday you won’t thank me for it.”

What it shows (our notes): Anakin's Chryya-class courier makes a suicidal reentry and melts a pursuing picket. "It’s not just about winning, Master ... It’s about winning with style." / "I could walk on water". Blinded droids use echolocation ("in one of the latest updates"). Anakin's Force-guided grenade collapses the caves ("think a little bit longer before rearranging the landscape").
> “Showing off! It’s not just about winning, Master. Federation attack droids coming in two files from the B-Seven landing sight: six, seven, eight of them,” he added carelessly, glancing at the Chryya’s tactical monitor. “It’s about winning with style.” He put his hand on the lightsaber at his side and prepared to launch himself out the Chryya’s forward hatch. “Ready?”
>
> Anakin hung in the air for an impossibly long time, let himself fall at last into a shoulder roll, two more shots at a droid trying to sneak up behind him, taking off its weapon hand and blowing out a knee, and then he was standing, perfectly balanced, with the blaster pistols steaming in the thin Vjun rain. “I could walk on water,” he said.
>
> “Echolocation. It’s a last-ditch backup directional sense—they’re squeaking like hawk-bats, trying to make an active sonar graph of the terrain.” Anakin gave him a look. “I’m not joking,” Obi-Wan said. “It was in one of the latest updates.”
>
> “You better hope not.” Seeing the young man’s stricken face, Obi-Wan relented. “I’m sure we would have felt it in the Force if Yoda had been killed. But next time, think a little bit longer before rearranging the landscape, would you?”
— Source: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous: notes part 4, Chapters 10–12.

What it shows (our notes): Anakin status details: his braid was "ritually severed after his audacious actions at Praesitlyn". His face is "scarred from a contest with Dooku-trained Asajj Ventress". He has a mechanical right hand in an "elbow-length glove". He is now a Jedi Knight, and Obi-Wan sits on the Council.
> Anakin used the honorific not as learner to teacher, but as Jedi Knight to Jedi Council member. The braid that had defined his earlier status had been ritually severed after his audacious actions at Praesitlyn. His tunic, knee-high boots, and tight-fitting trousers were as black as the night. His face scarred from a contest with Dooku-trained Asajj Ventress. His mechanical right hand sheathed in an elbow-length glove. He had let his hair grow long the past few months, falling almost to his shoulders now. His face he kept clean-shaven, unlike Obi-Wan, whose strong jaw was defined by a short beard.
— Source: Yoda: Dark Rendezvous: notes part 5, Del Rey back matter (text lines 2526–3870); Anakin status details: his braid was.

## The Dark Nest Trilogy (Troy Denning, Del Rey 2005): FULL consecutive re-read (2026-11-16 CST)

What it shows (our notes): Jacen with the Fallanassi. He is on a five-year search, through "The Jensaarai, the Aing-Tii, even the Witches of Dathomir" and the Theran Listeners. Akanah warns him:
> “I am sorry, but no.” Akanah’s eyes caught his gaze again and held it almost physically. “You have the same power I once sensed in your uncle Luke, but without the light. You must not leave before you have found some.”
>
> “Jedi serve the Force,” Jacen said. “The Force encompasses both light and dark.”
>
> “So now you are beyond light and dark?” Akanah asked. “Beyond good and evil?”
>
> “I’m no longer an active Jedi Knight,” Jacen answered, “but yes.”
>
> “And you do not understand the folly in that?” As Akanah spoke, her gaze seemed to grow deeper and darker. “The arrogance?”
>
> "With no light to guide you, Jacen, and the power I sense in you, I fear you will cause even more." (Akanah, JK prologue). The narration adds that she "truly feared that he would become an even greater monster than had his grandfather, Darth Vader." This foreshadows Caedus.
— Source: Dark Nest I: The Joiner King (Troy Denning, Del Rey 2005): notes part 1. POV: Akanah, about Darth Vader / Anakin Skywalker.

What it shows (our notes): The seven Jedi are Myrkr survivors: Jaina, Alema, Zekk, Lowbacca, Tesar, Tekli and Tahiri. "six young Jedi Knights had died in the process—including Anakin himself—and another was missing and presumed lost". This is Raynar.
> “I wish we knew,” Luke said. Undertaken in the middle of the war with the Yuuzhan Vong, the Myrkr mission had been as costly as it had been successful. Anakin Solo and his strike team had destroyed the enemy’s Jedi-killing voxyn. But six young Jedi Knights had died in the process—including Anakin himself—and another was missing and presumed lost. “All I can tell you is that for several weeks, Jaina and the other survivors of that mission reported feeling a ‘call’ from the Unknown Regions. On the day they left, that call became a cry for help.”
— Source: Dark Nest I: The Joiner King (Troy Denning, Del Rey 2005): notes part 1.

What it shows (our notes): Battle-meld aftereffect: "Their prolonged use of it on the Myrkr mission had weakened the boundaries among their minds" (Cilghal's diagnosis). This is why Jaina "had failed to reconnect with Jagged Fel". Anakin died "seven years later" (on Jaina's count).
> It had taken Cilghal only a few days to diagnose the problem as a delayed reaction to the Jedi battle-meld. Their prolonged use of it on the Myrkr mission had weakened the boundaries among their minds, with the result that now their emotions tended to fill the Force and blur together whenever they were close to each other. *(Cilghal's diagnosis)*
>
> Sometimes Jaina believed the side effect was also the reason so many strike team survivors found it difficult to move on with their lives. Tenel Ka was doing well as the Hapan queen, and Tekli and Tahiri seemed to regard Zonama Sekot as both a friend and a home, but the rest of them—Jaina, Alema, Zekk, Tesar, Lowbacca, even Jacen—still seemed lost, unable to maintain a connection with anyone who had not been there. Jaina knew that was why she had failed to reconnect with Jagged Fel during their desperate rendezvous when he had still been serving as Chiss liaison to the Galactic Alliance. She loved him, but she’d just grown increasingly distant from him. From everyone, really.
— Source: Dark Nest I: The Joiner King (Troy Denning, Del Rey 2005): notes part 1; Battle-meld aftereffect. POV: Battle-meld aftereffect, about Darth Vader / Anakin Skywalker.

What it shows (our notes): Key lore moment. Luke repairs a fault in R2-D2's deep-reserve memory chip, sector 222, and an encoded hologram plays:
> Luke caught a glimmer of moving light on the workbench. He flipped up his magnispecs, expecting to find the images of Tahiri and his dead nephew, Anakin, sharing a personal moment R2-D2 had caught with his holorecorder.
>
> Instead, Luke found himself watching a beautiful, hand-sized, brown-eyed woman whom he did not recognize. She walked across the workbench, then stopped beside a sinewy young man dressed, as she was, in nightclothes.
>
> “What’s bothering you?” she asked.
>
> The young man continued to look away from her. “Nothing.”
>
> “Anakin, how long is it going to take for us to be honest with each other?”
>
> Luke’s heart rocketed into his throat. He had not immediately recognized his father. He wanted to call out to Mara, to share with Leia what he was feeling … but he was too stunned. He simply continued watching.
>
> The young man—Anakin—turned to face the woman. “It was a dream.”
>
> “Bad?”
>
> Anakin looked over her head. “Like the ones I used to have about my mother … just before she died.”
>
> The woman hesitated, then finally asked, “And?”
>
> Anakin’s gaze fell. “It was about you.”
>
> The hologram crackled to an abrupt end, and an ominous humming arose deep inside R2-D2’s internal workings. Luke flipped down his magnispecs and peered in to find the recording head bumping against his soldering filament as it attempted to access sector 222.
>
> Anakin’s gaze fell. “It was about you.” *(JK ch. 18)*
— Source: Dark Nest I: The Joiner King: notes part 3 (ch. 18 onward).
(Excerpt widened 2026-11-17 to the relevant passage, word for word from The Joiner King (Troy Denning) (text copy `MEGA_Legend_The_Dark_Nest_Trilogy_Star_Wars_Dark_Nest_01_The_Joiner_King_Troy_Denning_epub`).)

What it shows (our notes): Jacen's presence stirs "in the place that had always belonged to her brother". Jaina wonders: "Would it truly be so bad if there was a war, and the Colony won? For once, there would be true galactic peace".
> Jaina felt a stirring deep down inside, in the place that had always belonged to her brother Jacen, and she knew he was with her, urging her to fight back, to throw off her lethargy, to break the Colony’s hold on her and reach for that part of her that was just Jaina.
>
> Jaina began to despair. Her anger was no match for the Will of the Colony. She had to find another way.
>
> Would it truly be so bad if there was a war, and the Colony won? For once, there would be true galactic peace—no vying for resources, no clashes of interest, no territorial conquests, just all the peoples of the galaxy working together for the common good. Was that so wrong?
— Source: Dark Nest I: The Joiner King: notes part 4 (end of ch. 29 onward); Jacen's presence stirs.

What it shows (our notes): Jaina muses about Jag joining: "the Dawn Rumble would be so much more—". Her first Wuluw is killed and the old rage comes back ("since the deaths of Anakin and Chewbacca"). Attrition math: "Kill one Killik, and ten thousand would take its place."
> And if Jag became a Joiner, she mused, the Dawn Rumble would be so much more—
>
> “R u u buruub!” Wuluw burst out. The little Killik started to turn and flee back down the tunnel. “Bur!”
>
> Jaina had seen—had caused—so much carnage in combat that she had believed herself numb to the storm of emotions it spawned. But the loss of the frightened little Wuluw brought it all back—all the fear and the anger and the guilt, the despair and the loneliness and the soul-scorching rage that had been lurking just beneath the surface since the deaths of Anakin and Chewbacca and so many others.
>
> A Killik could lay a thousand eggs a month, and within a year, those eggs would be battle-ready nymphs. In two years, the survivors would lay eggs of their own. Kill one Killik, and ten thousand would take its place. Kill ten thousand, and a million would take theirs. If the Chiss wanted to survive this war, they had only one choice: withdraw to their own borders and sue for peace. It was that simple.
— Source: *The Swarm War* notes part 2: ch. 10–; Jaina muses about Jag joining. POV: Jaina muses, about Darth Vader / Anakin Skywalker.

What it shows (our notes): The assassination debate. Kyp: "We just take out Raynar and the Unu" / "Raynar is a Jedi, and now he’s becoming a threat to the galaxy. He’s our responsibility". Corran: "taking out Raynar is murder." Zekk to Leia: "Because you’re afraid you’ll lose us the way you lost Anakin?" Saba on Gorog: "There is too much methane in them. They taste like a—".
> “Of course,” Kyp said. “We just take out Raynar and the Unu.”
>
> “Kill them?” Corran’s tone was shocked.
>
> “It doesn’t matter,” Kyp said. “You guys are really overthinking this. It’s simple: Raynar is a Jedi, and now he’s becoming a threat to the galaxy. He’s our responsibility, and we have to stop him. How we do that matters a lot less than whether we still can.”
>
> Corran Horn, as usual in matters of conscience, came to his answer more quickly than most. “War is one thing, but taking out Raynar is murder.”
>
> “Why?” Zekk demanded. “Because you’re afraid you’ll lose us the way you lost Anakin?”
>
> Coming from Zekk’s mouth instead of Jaina’s, the question felt just bizarre enough that the dagger of loss it drove into Leia’s chest did not find her heart. She retained her composure and studied her daughter’s image in silence, but Jaina was too tough to be stared down over the HoloNet. She simply accepted Leia’s glare with the unblinking eyes of a Joiner, then spoke in an even voice.
>
> “Good?” Saba sissed in amused disbelief. “You spent too much of your life in the spice minez, Kyp Durron. There is too much methane in them. They taste like a—”
— Source: Dark Nest II: *The Unseen Queen* (Troy Denning, 2005), notes part 1: front matter, prologue, ch. 1–5; The assassination debate. Kyp. POV: The assassination debate. Kyp, about Darth Vader / Anakin Skywalker.

> Very few people remember this now, but Star Wars appeared in only something like 32 theaters in the country when it first opened; yet every one of those theaters was showing a 70-millimeter print with 6-track Dolby Stereo, at a time when Dolby was brand-new and the 70mm format was practically extinct.
>
> I’d heard rumblings about Star Wars before it came out, through back channels, from film-savvy friends, but the general public had no idea what it was. Because of that, Star Wars had a sexy sort of street cred that other films didn’t. Save for Alec Guinness, the film’s lead actors were unknowns. But as I sat in the theater waiting for the lights to dim and the curtains to open, the feeling in the air was unmistakable: This was going to be something.
>
> “Something” was an understatement. What followed was probably the most amazing experience I’ve ever witnessed between a film and its audience.
>
> “Something” was an understatement. What followed was probably the most amazing experience I’ve ever witnessed between a film and its audience. Almost immediately, right after the Flash Gordon– inspired crawl, as the rebels’ ship was pursued by an endlessly enormous Imperial starcruiser … something happened that never happens in Oregon—the opening shot of a movie got an ovation.
>
> This wasn’t Hollywood, where knowledgeable industry people show appreciation for the efforts of their fellow co-workers. This was Oregon, where audiences had never, ever, in my experience (and I saw a lot of movies growing up), applauded a shot.
>
> This wasn’t Hollywood, where knowledgeable industry people show appreciation for the efforts of their fellow co-workers. This was Oregon, where audiences had never, ever, in my experience (and I saw a lot of movies growing up), applauded a shot.
>
> Though this is an extremely difficult thing for an actor to pull off (along with the growing formality of Luke’s speech as the trilogy goes on), Hamill handled it all with grace and commitment. Because Hamill believed completely, we did, too.
>
> But by now I was deep into the Star Wars saga. The first two films had set my expectations very high, and, truth be told, I had a few beefs with Jedi, which started when the opening crawl mentioned the Empire secretly beginning construction of a new armored battle station “even more powerful than the first dreaded Death Star.”
>
> More powerful? The first one could blow up a planet—how much “more powerful” could it get?
>
> There were other qualms. The disconnect between Jedi’s Luke Skywalker and Empire’s Luke, who had cut short his training against the advice of his Jedi mentors to save his friends, then (a) failed to save his friends; (b) got his ass kicked, losing a hand in the process; and (c) found out the most dastardly villain in the galaxy was also his dad.
>
> cocky dude who shows up in Jabba’s palace
> [fragment — full passage not on file]
>
> They won’t have to struggle, as George did, with overcoming either tremendous initial skepticism toward Star Wars or the creative challenge of chiseling it from the limitless white void of undefined possibilities. Instead, they will wrestle with the audience’s outsize expectations, or with the conflict between the artist’s fear of playing it too safely and the businessman’s fear of perceived risk to an “intellectual property” that is now proven to be extremely valuable.
>
> There will be more Star Wars films in the foreseeable future. But for many of us, it will always be about the original trilogy and the courageous journey of art and entrepreneurship that these films made possible—and which led to the creation of both an impressive body of films and innovative companies, such as Pixar, Industrial Light & Magic, Skywalker Sound, and THX.
>
> Now that George has extracted himself from his corporate responsibilities to once again enjoy the freedoms of an experimental filmmaker, it’s hard not to think of Luke Skywalker’s other father in the last shot of Return of the Jedi: finally freed of all the machinery …
>
> … and smiling.
>
> INTRODUCTION The making of each Star Wars film has mirrored the general mood of its respective cinematic chapter.
>
> … and smiling.
>
> INTRODUCTION The making of each Star Wars film has mirrored the general mood of its respective cinematic chapter. Episode IV was a scrappy battle pitting a small, resolute group of dreamers against the institutions of fear, which resulted in a euphoric victory for writer-director George Lucas and his creative collaborators.
>
> . . . and smiling.
— Source: The Making of Return of the Jedi (J. W. Rinzler, Del Rey 2013): notes part 1, front matter and ch. 1 (pp. 2–15).

Note (our notes, around the quote): Brad Bird foreword (pp. vi–vii): the Portland opening night of *Star Wars*, where "the opening shot of a movie got an ovation" and Vader was booed on sight. His beefs with *Jedi*: a second Death Star "even more powerful" ("The first one could blow up a planet—how much “more powerful” could it get?") and the "cocky dude who shows up in Jabba’s palace". He closes on Lucas, freed after the Disney sale, as "Luke Skywalker’s other father in the last shot of Return of the Jedi: finally freed of all the machinery . . . / . . . and smiling."
